If someone comes in later, they didn’t hear anything. And when I check the setup, everything looks the way it should. So to answer you… not really in the same way outside of work, at least that’s not what made me come here. It’s the voices there, when everything is supposed to be muted, that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear through the monitor speakers ever tell you to do something, or are they mostly criticizing you or making statements?\n\nPatient: They don’t usually tell me to do something exactly, not like a command. I’ll check the channels — sometimes I check them over and over — and then I still hear something through the monitor speakers. Not like static or a garbled sound, but words. My name, or things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nAnd no one else hears it. That’s the part that makes me feel… I don’t know, ashamed, I guess, and scared. Because I keep wanting it to be some technical issue, but when every channel is muted and nobody else reacts, I don’t know what to do with that. So, no, it’s not really shadows or figures that brought me here. It’s the voices through the monitors.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear through the monitor speakers are real, or could they be some kind of mistake or misperception on your part?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that, honestly. Part of me wants very badly for it to be a mistake — like a wiring issue, or some feed I didn’t mute correctly, or my brain filling in sounds because the room gets so quiet. I check the channels because I’m trying to prove it’s something ordinary.\n\nBut then it happens when every channel is muted. I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers clearly enough that it feels real in the moment, like someone is saying my name or saying, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.” And no one else hears anything. That’s what scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I know it doesn’t make sense if everything is muted and nobody else reacts. But when I’m sitting there and I hear it, it doesn’t feel like a vague misperception. It feels like it’s coming through the speakers. That’s why I came in, because I can’t tell myself anymore that it’s just nothing.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear these voices, aside from being scared and ashamed?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s this heavy feeling that I’m bad at the core of it, not just that I made a mistake. Like the voices hit something that’s already there, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen I hear them, I start replaying everything from my shift in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before making a call, every moment where I looked away for even a second. And it all starts to feel like evidence. Like, see, Ruth, you are careless. You are dangerous. You shouldn’t be trusted with people’s safety.\n\nAnd I know people make mistakes at work, I know that logically, but emotionally it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like if something goes wrong while I’m on watch, it would be my fault in this total, unforgivable way. Sometimes part of me even feels like I’d deserve to be punished for it, because if people got hurt because I missed something, then why should I get to just go home and act normal?\n\nSo aside from scared and ashamed… I guess I feel guilty. Worthless. Like I’m already guilty of something, even if I can’t point to exactly what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or hopeless when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gone to work or heard anything, it’s just there.\n\nAnd it can come over me in these ordinary moments too. I’ll be driving in for my shift and see the casino lights, and sometimes I’ll just start tearing up in the car. Not because something new happened right then, not because anyone said anything to me. I mean, I’ve had times in my life where I was stressed or down, sure, and I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But before this whole stretch — after the change with the surveillance lead and everything started feeling hopeless and guilty all the time — I had never heard accusations coming through equipment like that.\n\nI’d never had it feel like the intercom or the monitors or whatever was… saying things about me. I don’t always have a clear “this is what I did” kind of reason, which almost makes it worse, because then I start searching for one.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll replay things over and over in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I didn’t call something in fast enough, every moment where I think maybe I looked away too long or didn’t notice something I should have. Like if someone got hurt or something bad happened, it would somehow be because I wasn’t careful enough.\n\nI know saying it out loud sounds extreme, but inside it doesn’t feel extreme. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. I’ve had moments where I’m distracted, like I’m doing something automatically or I’m too tired to think clearly, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a whole day where it wasn’t there.

Most days it’s like this heavy gray mood settles over me before I even get out of bed. I’ll wake up and it’s already there, like I’m starting the day from underneath something. And then by the time I’m driving in for my shift, it can get worse in this really stupid-feeling way — like I’ll see the casino lights, just the lights outside, and I can feel myself tearing up in the car. Not because anything specific happened right then. Just… seeing them, knowing I’m going in, feeling that weight.

So no, in the past two weeks, I can’t think of a day where I felt okay or actually interested in things again. It’s been pretty much every day.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what finally made me come in is something that’s been happening at work, and it scared me enough that I couldn’t keep telling myself it was just stress or the equipment acting up.\n\nI work in surveillance, so I’m in this room with all the monitors, and there are long quiet stretches where it’s just me watching the screens. And sometimes, even when I know the channels are muted — like I’ve checked them, I’ve actually looked and made sure — I hear voices coming through the monitor speakers. Not loud like someone yelling, but clear enough that I can make out words. They’ll say things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nAnd I know how that sounds. I do. That’s why I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud. But no one else seems to hear it, and it happens when everything is supposed to be muted. I’ll sit there staring at the screens trying to figure out if I missed some audio feed or if there’s interference or something, but… it feels like it’s directed right at me. That’s what frightened me. I don’t feel like I can just ignore it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear these voices when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work. That’s where it’s been happening, in the surveillance room, during those really quiet stretches when I’m alone with the monitors and it gets so still that every little hum from the equipment stands out.\n\nI don’t mean I’m listening to an open feed by mistake — I check. I’ll look at the controls, make sure the channels are muted, sometimes more than once, because I’m trying to prove to myself there’s some ordinary explanation. But then I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers anyway, like a voice just cutting through the quiet and saying my name or saying something like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nAnd the part that really gets to me is that no one else reacts. If someone comes in later, they didn’t hear anything. And when I check the setup, everything looks the way it should. So to answer you… not really in the same way outside of work, at least that’s not what made me come here. It’s the voices there, when everything is supposed to be muted, that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear through the monitor speakers ever tell you to do something, or are they mostly criticizing you or making statements?\n\nPatient: They don’t usually tell me to do something exactly, not like a command. It’s more… accusing me, I guess. Criticizing me, or making these statements that feel like they’re aimed right at me.\n\nLike I’ll be in the surveillance room and everything is quiet, and I’ve already checked that the channels are muted — sometimes I check more than once because I’m trying to catch myself being wrong, you know? And then I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers anyway. Things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nSo it’s not really, “Go do this” or “Do that.” It’s more like they’re saying I’ve already failed, or that something bad is going to happen because I didn’t notice something. That’s the part that scares me, because it feels so specific, and no one else hears it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't there, like shadows or figures, especially when you're in the surveillance room?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve seen, like, an actual figure standing there or anything I could point to and say, “That wasn’t real.” The thing that really scared me enough to come in was the voices.\n\nIt happens during those quiet stretches in the surveillance room, when I’m alone with all the monitors and everything is supposed to be muted. I’ll check the channels — sometimes I check them over and over — and then I still hear something through the monitor speakers. Not like static or a garbled sound, but words. My name, or things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nAnd no one else hears it. That’s the part that makes me feel… I don’t know, ashamed, I guess, and scared. Because I keep wanting it to be some technical issue, but when every channel is muted and nobody else reacts, I don’t know what to do with that. So, no, it’s not really shadows or figures that brought me here. It’s the voices through the monitors.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear through the monitor speakers are real, or could they be some kind of mistake or misperception on your part?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that, honestly. Part of me wants very badly for it to be a mistake — like a wiring issue, or some feed I didn’t mute correctly, or my brain filling in sounds because the room gets so quiet. I check the channels because I’m trying to prove it’s something ordinary.\n\nBut then it happens when every channel is muted. I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers clearly enough that it feels real in the moment, like someone is saying my name or saying, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.” And no one else hears anything. That’s what scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I know it doesn’t make sense if everything is muted and nobody else reacts. But when I’m sitting there and I hear it, it doesn’t feel like a vague misperception. It feels like it’s coming through the speakers. That’s why I came in, because I can’t tell myself anymore that it’s just nothing.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear these voices, aside from being scared and ashamed?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s this heavy feeling that I’m bad at the core of it, not just that I made a mistake. Like the voices hit something that’s already there, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen I hear them, I start replaying everything from my shift in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before making a call, every moment where I looked away for even a second. And it all starts to feel like evidence. Like, see, Ruth, you are careless. You are dangerous. You shouldn’t be trusted with people’s safety.\n\nAnd I know people make mistakes at work, I know that logically, but emotionally it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like if something goes wrong while I’m on watch, it would be my fault in this total, unforgivable way. Sometimes part of me even feels like I’d deserve to be punished for it, because if people got hurt because I missed something, then why should I get to just go home and act normal?\n\nSo aside from scared and ashamed… I guess I feel guilty. Worthless. Like I’m already guilty of something, even if I can’t point to exactly what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or hopeless when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not only when the voices happen. Most days I feel like… like this heavy gray mood has already settled on me before I even get out of bed. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gone to work or heard anything, it’s just there.\n\nAnd it can come over me in these ordinary moments too. I’ll be driving in for my shift and see the casino lights, and sometimes I’ll just start tearing up in the car. Not because something new happened right then, not because anyone said anything to me. Just seeing the place, knowing I have to go in, knowing I’m going to sit there under those lights and monitors… it feels like too much.\n\nSo yes, sad and hopeless is probably fair. It’s like the day is already ruined before it starts, and I don’t even know how to explain that without sounding dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant losses or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way people usually mean, like a recent death or an assault or some huge accident that I can point to and say, “That’s when everything changed.”\n\nI keep trying to think if there’s something I’m minimizing, because when you ask it that way I feel like maybe there’s supposed to be an answer. But no, I haven’t had some major loss recently. My life has had stressful things, like anybody’s, but I can’t honestly say there’s been one big traumatic event that explains all of this.\n\nUnless you mean work stress in general, or just years of being responsible for watching everything and worrying you missed something. But I don’t know if that counts as trauma. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. So… no, I guess not anything significant that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience like this before, where you heard voices or had other unusual perceptions?\n\nPatient: No… not like this. I mean, I’ve had times in my life where I was stressed or down, sure, and I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But before this whole stretch — after the change with the surveillance lead and everything started feeling hopeless and guilty all the time — I had never heard accusations coming through equipment like that.\n\nI’d never had it feel like the intercom or the monitors or whatever was… saying things about me. And I’d never felt so certain, in that awful way, that I was dangerous to everyone around me or that I couldn’t be trusted. That part feels new, and it seems tied up with this same period where I’ve been so low.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember anything like voices before this. Not in childhood, not earlier in adulthood, not even at other stressful jobs. This feels like it came with this particular low point, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you deserve to be punished for something, even if you're not sure what it is?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always have a clear “this is what I did” kind of reason, which almost makes it worse, because then I start searching for one.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll replay things over and over in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I didn’t call something in fast enough, every moment where I think maybe I looked away too long or didn’t notice something I should have. And then it stops feeling like normal mistakes or normal human limits. It starts feeling like proof that I’m careless, or that I’m dangerous to have in that job.\n\nAnd if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if I don’t know that I caused it, part of me feels like I deserve whatever punishment comes from that. Like I should be blamed. Like if someone got hurt or something bad happened, it would somehow be because I wasn’t careful enough.\n\nI know saying it out loud sounds extreme, but inside it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like… like I’m waiting to be found out for being irresponsible, even when I can’t point to one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or worthless all the time, or does it mostly happen when you're hearing the voices or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s more than just when I’m hearing something, I think. The voices or the work stuff make it sharper, like it gets louder in my head, but the guilt is kind of there most of the time now.\n\nI keep going back over things, especially from work — like every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before calling something in, every moment where I think I should’ve noticed more. And I don’t just think, “Oh, that was a mistake.” It turns into, “That proves I’m careless,” or “I’m dangerous to have watching anything important.”\n\nSo when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. But even away from work, it lingers. I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back there in my head, replaying it again, trying to figure out what I missed. And if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if nobody has said it was my fault, part of me feels like I deserve to be punished for it. Like I should be held responsible because I should have prevented it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I used to have things I actually looked forward to. Salsa class was a big one — I’d be thinking about it during the week, what shoes to wear, whether I was getting better at a turn, that kind of thing. And softball, too. Even if I was tired, once I got there I’d usually be glad I went.\n\nI used to like building PCs, messing around with parts, watching videos about setups, comparing components — it was this satisfying thing where I could focus and feel like I was making something work. And I’d have people over for board games sometimes, which sounds small, but I really did enjoy setting it up, picking games, making snacks, all of that.\n\nLately I just… don’t answer. People text about salsa or softball or ask if I’m coming, or someone says we should do a game night, and I just let it sit there. I see the message, and I know the old version of me would’ve responded, but now it’s like there’s this wall between me and it. I just stay home. I don’t even really miss it in the normal way — it’s more like none of it can reach me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not just that I’m busy or tired or forgetting to go. It’s more like the part of me that used to want those things has gone quiet.\n\nLike salsa class — I used to actually look forward to it. I’d think about it ahead of time, and even if I was nervous or had a long day, once I was there I could feel myself sort of come alive a little. Same with softball. I wasn’t always amazing or anything, but I liked showing up, being part of it.\n\nAnd building PCs used to really pull me in. I could spend hours comparing parts or watching videos or planning a setup, and it felt satisfying. Now I’ll maybe see something about it online and just scroll past it. It doesn’t hook me.\n\nBoard games too… I used to have people over and enjoy planning it, picking the game, setting snacks out, making it feel like a little event. Now when people text me about getting together, I mostly just ignore it. I see the messages sitting there, and I know I should answer, but I don’t. I just stay home.\n\nIt’s like those things are still there, but they don’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy gray mood that you mentioned?\n\nPatient: I think… about four months now. It’s hard to pin down exactly because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting, or maybe I was just tired from the change.\n\nBut it really started after I got moved into the lead casino surveillance role. That’s when the gray feeling began to settle in more and more. At first it was maybe just some bad days, but then it became most days, and then it felt like I couldn’t really get out from under it. So… yeah, roughly four months. Since that move happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy doing anything that brings you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. I keep trying to think of something, because it feels strange to say “nothing,” like I’m being dramatic, but the things that used to make me feel like myself just don’t do that anymore.\n\nSalsa used to be a real thing for me — I’d look forward to it, plan around it, get a little nervous in a good way, you know? Softball too, even just being around the team and having a reason to show up somewhere. And I used to love building PCs, like comparing parts and getting absorbed in it for hours. Board game nights were another one… I liked having people over, setting everything up, making snacks, picking the right game.\n\nNow people text me about those things, and I just… leave it. I see the messages. I know they’re there. I’ll even open them sometimes and then close them again. And then I just sit at home. It’s like I can remember that those things used to matter, but I can’t feel it anymore. None of it really reaches me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you've lost interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months, I’d say. I know I mentioned the mood part started around then too, but the losing interest kind of came along with it — maybe not all at once, but that’s when I started noticing I wasn’t reaching for things anymore.\n\nIt was after I got moved into the lead casino surveillance role. At first I thought I was just adjusting or overwhelmed, and I kept telling myself, “Okay, once I get used to the new position I’ll feel like going back to things.” But then weeks kept passing, and I still wasn’t going to salsa, wasn’t showing up for softball, wasn’t getting excited about PC stuff or board game nights.\n\nSo… roughly four months. Since that role change.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. It’s been much longer than two weeks. I mean, I almost wish it were just a couple of weeks, because then I could tell myself it was a bad stretch or something temporary, but it’s been about four months now.\n\nIt really started around when I was moved into the lead casino surveillance role. Since then, the depressed feeling and the not caring about things I used to care about have been there most of the day, almost every day. There might be little moments where I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s just been… there, kind of sitting on me, since that change happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you didn't feel depressed or lose interest in activities?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I’ve had moments where I’m distracted, like I’m doing something automatically or I’m too tired to think clearly, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a whole day where it wasn’t there.\n\nMost days it’s like this heavy gray mood settles over me before I even get out of bed. I’ll wake up and it’s already there, like I’m starting the day from underneath something. And then by the time I’m driving in for my shift, it can get worse in this really stupid-feeling way — like I’ll see the casino lights, just the lights outside, and I can feel myself tearing up in the car. Not because anything specific happened right then. Just… seeing them, knowing I’m going in, feeling that weight.\n\nSo no, in the past two weeks, I can’t think of a day where I felt okay or actually interested in things again. It’s been pretty much every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s like the day is already ruined before it starts, and I don’t even know how to explain that without sounding dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant losses or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way people usually mean, like a recent death or an assault or some huge accident that I can point to and say, “That’s when everything changed.”\n\nI keep trying to think if there’s something I’m minimizing, because when you ask it that way I feel like maybe there’s supposed to be an answer. But no, I haven’t had some major loss recently. My life has had stressful things, like anybody’s, but I can’t honestly say there’s been one big traumatic event that explains all of this.\n\nUnless you mean work stress in general, or just years of being responsible for watching everything and worrying you missed something. But I don’t know if that counts as trauma. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. So… no, I guess not anything significant that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience like this before, where you heard voices or had other unusual perceptions?\n\nPatient: No… not like this. I mean, I’ve had times in my life where I was stressed or down, sure, and I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But before this whole stretch — after the change with the surveillance lead and everything started feeling hopeless and guilty all the time — I had never heard accusations coming through equipment like that.\n\nI’d never had it feel like the intercom or the monitors or whatever was… saying things about me. And I’d never felt so certain, in that awful way, that I was dangerous to everyone around me or that I couldn’t be trusted. That part feels new, and it seems tied up with this same period where I’ve been so low.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember anything like voices before this. Not in childhood, not earlier in adulthood, not even at other stressful jobs. This feels like it came with this particular low point, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you deserve to be punished for something, even if you're not sure what it is?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always have a clear “this is what I did” kind of reason, which almost makes it worse, because then I start searching for one.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll replay things over and over in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I didn’t call something in fast enough, every moment where I think maybe I looked away too long or didn’t notice something I should have. And then it stops feeling like normal mistakes or normal human limits. It starts feeling like proof that I’m careless, or that I’m dangerous to have in that job.\n\nAnd if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if I don’t know that I caused it, part of me feels like I deserve whatever punishment comes from that. Like I should be blamed. Like if someone got hurt or something bad happened, it would somehow be because I wasn’t careful enough.\n\nI know saying it out loud sounds extreme, but inside it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like… like I’m waiting to be found out for being irresponsible, even when I can’t point to one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or worthless all the time, or does it mostly happen when you're hearing the voices or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s more than just when I’m hearing something, I think. The voices or the work stuff make it sharper, like it gets louder in my head, but the guilt is kind of there most of the time now.\n\nI keep going back over things, especially from work — like every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before calling something in, every moment where I think I should’ve noticed more. And I don’t just think, “Oh, that was a mistake.” It turns into, “That proves I’m careless,” or “I’m dangerous to have watching anything important.”\n\nSo when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. But even away from work, it lingers. I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back there in my head, replaying it again, trying to figure out what I missed. And if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if nobody has said it was my fault, part of me feels like I deserve to be punished for it. Like I should be held responsible because I should have prevented it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I used to have things I actually looked forward to. Salsa class was a big one — I’d be thinking about it during the week, what shoes to wear, whether I was getting better at a turn, that kind of thing. And softball, too. Even if I was tired, once I got there I’d usually be glad I went.\n\nI used to like building PCs, messing around with parts, watching videos about setups, comparing components — it was this satisfying thing where I could focus and feel like I was making something work. And I’d have people over for board games sometimes, which sounds small, but I really did enjoy setting it up, picking games, making snacks, all of that.\n\nLately I just… don’t answer. People text about salsa or softball or ask if I’m coming, or someone says we should do a game night, and I just let it sit there. I see the message, and I know the old version of me would’ve responded, but now it’s like there’s this wall between me and it. I just stay home. I don’t even really miss it in the normal way — it’s more like none of it can reach me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not just that I’m busy or tired or forgetting to go. It’s more like the part of me that used to want those things has gone quiet.\n\nLike salsa class — I used to actually look forward to it. I’d think about it ahead of time, and even if I was nervous or had a long day, once I was there I could feel myself sort of come alive a little. Same with softball. I wasn’t always amazing or anything, but I liked showing up, being part of it.\n\nAnd building PCs used to really pull me in. I could spend hours comparing parts or watching videos or planning a setup, and it felt satisfying. Now I’ll maybe see something about it online and just scroll past it. It doesn’t hook me.\n\nBoard games too… I used to have people over and enjoy planning it, picking the game, setting snacks out, making it feel like a little event. Now when people text me about getting together, I mostly just ignore it. I see the messages sitting there, and I know I should answer, but I don’t. I just stay home.\n\nIt’s like those things are still there, but they don’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy gray mood that you mentioned?\n\nPatient: I think… about four months now. It’s hard to pin down exactly because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting, or maybe I was just tired from the change.\n\nBut it really started after I got moved into the lead casino surveillance role. That’s when the gray feeling began to settle in more and more. At first it was maybe just some bad days, but then it became most days, and then it felt like I couldn’t really get out from under it. So… yeah, roughly four months. Since that move happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy doing anything that brings you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. I keep trying to think of something, because it feels strange to say “nothing,” like I’m being dramatic, but the things that used to make me feel like myself just don’t do that anymore.\n\nSalsa used to be a real thing for me — I’d look forward to it, plan around it, get a little nervous in a good way, you know? Softball too, even just being around the team and having a reason to show up somewhere. And I used to love building PCs, like comparing parts and getting absorbed in it for hours. Board game nights were another one… I liked having people over, setting everything up, making snacks, picking the right game.\n\nNow people text me about those things, and I just… leave it. It’s like I can remember that those things used to matter, but I can’t feel it anymore. Criticizing me, or making these statements that feel like they’re aimed right at me.\n\nLike I’ll be in the surveillance room and everything is quiet, and I’ve already checked that the channels are muted — sometimes I check more than once because I’m trying to catch myself being wrong, you know? Things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nSo it’s not really, “Go do this” or “Do that.” It’s more like they’re saying I’ve already failed, or that something bad is going to happen because I didn’t notice something. Because I keep wanting it to be some technical issue, but when every channel is muted and nobody else reacts, I don’t know what to do with that. So, no, it’s not really shadows or figures that brought me here. It’s the voices through the monitors.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear through the monitor speakers are real, or could they be some kind of mistake or misperception on your part?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that, honestly. Part of me wants very badly for it to be a mistake — like a wiring issue, or some feed I didn’t mute correctly, or my brain filling in sounds because the room gets so quiet. I check the channels because I’m trying to prove it’s something ordinary.\n\nBut then it happens when every channel is muted. I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers clearly enough that it feels real in the moment, like someone is saying my name or saying, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.” And no one else hears anything. That’s what scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I know it doesn’t make sense if everything is muted and nobody else reacts. But when I’m sitting there and I hear it, it doesn’t feel like a vague misperception. It feels like it’s coming through the speakers. That’s why I came in, because I can’t tell myself anymore that it’s just nothing.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear these voices, aside from being scared and ashamed?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s this heavy feeling that I’m bad at the core of it, not just that I made a mistake. Like the voices hit something that’s already there, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen I hear them, I start replaying everything from my shift in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before making a call, every moment where I looked away for even a second. And it all starts to feel like evidence. Like, see, Ruth, you are careless. You are dangerous. You shouldn’t be trusted with people’s safety.\n\nAnd I know people make mistakes at work, I know that logically, but emotionally it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like if something goes wrong while I’m on watch, it would be my fault in this total, unforgivable way. Sometimes part of me even feels like I’d deserve to be punished for it, because if people got hurt because I missed something, then why should I get to just go home and act normal?\n\nSo aside from scared and ashamed… I guess I feel guilty. Worthless. Like I’m already guilty of something, even if I can’t point to exactly what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or hopeless when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not only when the voices happen. Most days I feel like… like this heavy gray mood has already settled on me before I even get out of bed. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gone to work or heard anything, it’s just there.\n\nAnd it can come over me in these ordinary moments too. I’ll be driving in for my shift and see the casino lights, and sometimes I’ll just start tearing up in the car. Not because something new happened right then, not because anyone said anything to me. Just seeing the place, knowing I have to go in, knowing I’m going to sit there under those lights and monitors… it feels like too much.\n\nSo yes, sad and hopeless is probably fair. It’s like the day is already ruined before it starts, and I don’t even know how to explain that without sounding dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant losses or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way people usually mean, like a recent death or an assault or some huge accident that I can point to and say, “That’s when everything changed.”\n\nI keep trying to think if there’s something I’m minimizing, because when you ask it that way I feel like maybe there’s supposed to be an answer. But no, I haven’t had some major loss recently. My life has had stressful things, like anybody’s, but I can’t honestly say there’s been one big traumatic event that explains all of this.\n\nUnless you mean work stress in general, or just years of being responsible for watching everything and worrying you missed something. But I don’t know if that counts as trauma. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. So… no, I guess not anything significant that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience like this before, where you heard voices or had other unusual perceptions?\n\nPatient: No… not like this. I mean, I’ve had times in my life where I was stressed or down, sure, and I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But before this whole stretch — after the change with the surveillance lead and everything started feeling hopeless and guilty all the time — I had never heard accusations coming through equipment like that.\n\nI’d never had it feel like the intercom or the monitors or whatever was… saying things about me. And I’d never felt so certain, in that awful way, that I was dangerous to everyone around me or that I couldn’t be trusted. That part feels new, and it seems tied up with this same period where I’ve been so low.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember anything like voices before this. Not in childhood, not earlier in adulthood, not even at other stressful jobs. This feels like it came with this particular low point, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you deserve to be punished for something, even if you're not sure what it is?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always have a clear “this is what I did” kind of reason, which almost makes it worse, because then I start searching for one.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll replay things over and over in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I didn’t call something in fast enough, every moment where I think maybe I looked away too long or didn’t notice something I should have. And then it stops feeling like normal mistakes or normal human limits. It starts feeling like proof that I’m careless, or that I’m dangerous to have in that job.\n\nAnd if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if I don’t know that I caused it, part of me feels like I deserve whatever punishment comes from that. Like I should be blamed. Like if someone got hurt or something bad happened, it would somehow be because I wasn’t careful enough.\n\nI know saying it out loud sounds extreme, but inside it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like… like I’m waiting to be found out for being irresponsible, even when I can’t point to one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or worthless all the time, or does it mostly happen when you're hearing the voices or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s more than just when I’m hearing something, I think. The voices or the work stuff make it sharper, like it gets louder in my head, but the guilt is kind of there most of the time now.\n\nI keep going back over things, especially from work — like every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before calling something in, every moment where I think I should’ve noticed more. And I don’t just think, “Oh, that was a mistake.” It turns into, “That proves I’m careless,” or “I’m dangerous to have watching anything important.”\n\nSo when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. But even away from work, it lingers. I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back there in my head, replaying it again, trying to figure out what I missed. And if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if nobody has said it was my fault, part of me feels like I deserve to be punished for it. Like I should be held responsible because I should have prevented it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I used to have things I actually looked forward to. Salsa class was a big one — I’d be thinking about it during the week, what shoes to wear, whether I was getting better at a turn, that kind of thing. And softball, too. Even if I was tired, once I got there I’d usually be glad I went.\n\nI used to like building PCs, messing around with parts, watching videos about setups, comparing components — it was this satisfying thing where I could focus and feel like I was making something work. And I’d have people over for board games sometimes, which sounds small, but I really did enjoy setting it up, picking games, making snacks, all of that.\n\nLately I just… don’t answer. People text about salsa or softball or ask if I’m coming, or someone says we should do a game night, and I just let it sit there. I see the message, and I know the old version of me would’ve responded, but now it’s like there’s this wall between me and it. I just stay home. I don’t even really miss it in the normal way — it’s more like none of it can reach me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not just that I’m busy or tired or forgetting to go. It’s more like the part of me that used to want those things has gone quiet.\n\nLike salsa class — I used to actually look forward to it. I’d think about it ahead of time, and even if I was nervous or had a long day, once I was there I could feel myself sort of come alive a little. Same with softball. I wasn’t always amazing or anything, but I liked showing up, being part of it.\n\nAnd building PCs used to really pull me in. I could spend hours comparing parts or watching videos or planning a setup, and it felt satisfying. Now I’ll maybe see something about it online and just scroll past it. It doesn’t hook me.\n\nBoard games too… I used to have people over and enjoy planning it, picking the game, setting snacks out, making it feel like a little event. Now when people text me about getting together, I mostly just ignore it. I see the messages sitting there, and I know I should answer, but I don’t. I just stay home.\n\nIt’s like those things are still there, but they don’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy gray mood that you mentioned?\n\nPatient: I think… about four months now. It’s hard to pin down exactly because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting, or maybe I was just tired from the change.\n\nBut it really started after I got moved into the lead casino surveillance role. That’s when the gray feeling began to settle in more and more. At first it was maybe just some bad days, but then it became most days, and then it felt like I couldn’t really get out from under it. So… yeah, roughly four months. Since that move happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy doing anything that brings you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. I keep trying to think of something, because it feels strange to say “nothing,” like I’m being dramatic, but the things that used to make me feel like myself just don’t do that anymore.\n\nSalsa used to be a real thing for me — I’d look forward to it, plan around it, get a little nervous in a good way, you know? Softball too, even just being around the team and having a reason to show up somewhere. And I used to love building PCs, like comparing parts and getting absorbed in it for hours. Board game nights were another one… I liked having people over, setting everything up, making snacks, picking the right game.\n\nNow people text me about those things, and I just… leave it. I see the messages. I know they’re there. I’ll even open them sometimes and then close them again. And then I just sit at home. It’s like I can remember that those things used to matter, but I can’t feel it anymore. None of it really reaches me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you've lost interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months, I’d say. I know I mentioned the mood part started around then too, but the losing interest kind of came along with it — maybe not all at once, but that’s when I started noticing I wasn’t reaching for things anymore.\n\nIt was after I got moved into the lead casino surveillance role. At first I thought I was just adjusting or overwhelmed, and I kept telling myself, “Okay, once I get used to the new position I’ll feel like going back to things.” But then weeks kept passing, and I still wasn’t going to salsa, wasn’t showing up for softball, wasn’t getting excited about PC stuff or board game nights.\n\nSo… roughly four months. Since that role change.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. It’s been much longer than two weeks. Since then, the depressed feeling and the not caring about things I used to care about have been there most of the day, almost every day. There might be little moments where I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s just been… there, kind of sitting on me, since that change happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you didn't feel depressed or lose interest in activities?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I’ve had moments where I’m distracted, like I’m doing something automatically or I’m too tired to think clearly, but I wouldn’t say there’s been a whole day where it wasn’t there.\n\nMost days it’s like this heavy gray mood settles over me before I even get out of bed. I’ll wake up and it’s already there, like I’m starting the day from underneath something. And then by the time I’m driving in for my shift, it can get worse in this really stupid-feeling way — like I’ll see the casino lights, just the lights outside, and I can feel myself tearing up in the car. I guess I haven’t been tracking it closely. I don’t think there’s been some dramatic shift that I could point to. I see the messages. I know they’re there. I’ll even open them sometimes and then close them again. And then I just sit at home. Yes. I mean… yes, most of every day. It’s not like I wake up neutral and then something happens. Most mornings it’s already there before I even get out of bed, like this heavy gray mood has settled over me overnight and I’m just… starting the day underneath it.

And sometimes, even when I know the channels are muted — like I’ve checked them, I’ve actually looked and made sure — I hear voices coming through the monitor speakers. Not loud like someone yelling, but clear enough that I can make out words. They’ll say things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nAnd I know how that sounds. I do. That’s why I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud. I’ll sit there staring at the screens trying to figure out if I missed some audio feed or if there’s interference or something, but… it feels like it’s directed right at me. That’s what frightened me. I don’t feel like I can just ignore it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear these voices when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work. That’s where it’s been happening, in the surveillance room, during those really quiet stretches when I’m alone with the monitors and it gets so still that every little hum from the equipment stands out.\n\nI don’t mean I’m listening to an open feed by mistake — I check. I’ll look at the controls, make sure the channels are muted, sometimes more than once, because I’m trying to prove to myself there’s some ordinary explanation. But then I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers anyway, like a voice just cutting through the quiet and saying my name or saying something like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nAnd the part that really gets to me is that no one else reacts. If someone comes in later, they didn’t hear anything. And when I check the setup, everything looks the way it should. So to answer you… not really in the same way outside of work, at least that’s not what made me come here. It’s the voices there, when everything is supposed to be muted, that scared me.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear through the monitor speakers ever tell you to do something, or are they mostly criticizing you or making statements?\n\nPatient: They don’t usually tell me to do something exactly, not like a command. It’s more… accusing me, I guess. Criticizing me, or making these statements that feel like they’re aimed right at me.\n\nLike I’ll be in the surveillance room and everything is quiet, and I’ve already checked that the channels are muted — sometimes I check more than once because I’m trying to catch myself being wrong, you know? And then I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers anyway. Things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nSo it’s not really, “Go do this” or “Do that.” It’s more like they’re saying I’ve already failed, or that something bad is going to happen because I didn’t notice something. That’s the part that scares me, because it feels so specific, and no one else hears it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't there, like shadows or figures, especially when you're in the surveillance room?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve seen, like, an actual figure standing there or anything I could point to and say, “That wasn’t real.” The thing that really scared me enough to come in was the voices.\n\nIt happens during those quiet stretches in the surveillance room, when I’m alone with all the monitors and everything is supposed to be muted. I’ll check the channels — sometimes I check them over and over — and then I still hear something through the monitor speakers. Not like static or a garbled sound, but words. My name, or things like, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.”\n\nAnd no one else hears it. That’s the part that makes me feel… I don’t know, ashamed, I guess, and scared. Because I keep wanting it to be some technical issue, but when every channel is muted and nobody else reacts, I don’t know what to do with that. So, no, it’s not really shadows or figures that brought me here. It’s the voices through the monitors.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear through the monitor speakers are real, or could they be some kind of mistake or misperception on your part?\n\nPatient: I keep going back and forth on that, honestly. Part of me wants very badly for it to be a mistake — like a wiring issue, or some feed I didn’t mute correctly, or my brain filling in sounds because the room gets so quiet. I check the channels because I’m trying to prove it’s something ordinary.\n\nBut then it happens when every channel is muted. I’ll hear it through the monitor speakers clearly enough that it feels real in the moment, like someone is saying my name or saying, “You missed it, Ruth,” or, “People are going to get hurt because of you.” And no one else hears anything. That’s what scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I know it doesn’t make sense if everything is muted and nobody else reacts. But when I’m sitting there and I hear it, it doesn’t feel like a vague misperception. It feels like it’s coming through the speakers. That’s why I came in, because I can’t tell myself anymore that it’s just nothing.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear these voices, aside from being scared and ashamed?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s this heavy feeling that I’m bad at the core of it, not just that I made a mistake. Like the voices hit something that’s already there, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen I hear them, I start replaying everything from my shift in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before making a call, every moment where I looked away for even a second. And it all starts to feel like evidence. Like, see, Ruth, you are careless. You are dangerous. You shouldn’t be trusted with people’s safety.\n\nAnd I know people make mistakes at work, I know that logically, but emotionally it doesn’t feel like that. It feels like if something goes wrong while I’m on watch, it would be my fault in this total, unforgivable way. Sometimes part of me even feels like I’d deserve to be punished for it, because if people got hurt because I missed something, then why should I get to just go home and act normal?\n\nSo aside from scared and ashamed… I guess I feel guilty. Worthless. Like I’m already guilty of something, even if I can’t point to exactly what happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel sad or hopeless when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not only when the voices happen. Most days I feel like… like this heavy gray mood has already settled on me before I even get out of bed. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gone to work or heard anything, it’s just there.\n\nAnd it can come over me in these ordinary moments too. I’ll be driving in for my shift and see the casino lights, and sometimes I’ll just start tearing up in the car. Not because something new happened right then, not because anyone said anything to me. Just seeing the place, knowing I have to go in, knowing I’m going to sit there under those lights and monitors… it feels like too much.\n\nSo yes, sad and hopeless is probably fair. It’s like the day is already ruined before it starts, and I don’t even know how to explain that without sounding dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant losses or traumatic events in your life recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. Not in the way people usually mean, like a recent death or an assault or some huge accident that I can point to and say, “That’s when everything changed.”\n\nI keep trying to think if there’s something I’m minimizing, because when you ask it that way I feel like maybe there’s supposed to be an answer. But no, I haven’t had some major loss recently. My life has had stressful things, like anybody’s, but I can’t honestly say there’s been one big traumatic event that explains all of this.\n\nUnless you mean work stress in general, or just years of being responsible for watching everything and worrying you missed something. But I don’t know if that counts as trauma. I wouldn’t want to overstate it. So… no, I guess not anything significant that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an experience like this before, where you heard voices or had other unusual perceptions?\n\nPatient: No… not like this. I mean, I’ve had times in my life where I was stressed or down, sure, and I can be hard on myself, probably more than I should be. But before this whole stretch — after the change with the surveillance lead and everything started feeling hopeless and guilty all the time — I had never heard accusations coming through equipment like that.\n\nI’d never had it feel like the intercom or the monitors or whatever was… saying things about me. And I’d never felt so certain, in that awful way, that I was dangerous to everyone around me or that I couldn’t be trusted. That part feels new, and it seems tied up with this same period where I’ve been so low.\n\nSo no, I don’t remember anything like voices before this. Not in childhood, not earlier in adulthood, not even at other stressful jobs. This feels like it came with this particular low point, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you deserve to be punished for something, even if you're not sure what it is?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t always have a clear “this is what I did” kind of reason, which almost makes it worse, because then I start searching for one.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll replay things over and over in my head — every camera angle I might have missed, every time I didn’t call something in fast enough, every moment where I think maybe I looked away too long or didn’t notice something I should have. And then it stops feeling like normal mistakes or normal human limits. It starts feeling like proof that I’m careless, or that I’m dangerous to have in that job.\n\nAnd if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if I don’t know that I caused it, part of me feels like I deserve whatever punishment comes from that. Like I should be blamed. Like if someone got hurt or something bad happened, it would somehow be because I wasn’t careful enough.\n\nI know saying it out loud sounds extreme, but inside it doesn’t feel extreme. It feels like… like I’m waiting to be found out for being irresponsible, even when I can’t point to one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or worthless all the time, or does it mostly happen when you're hearing the voices or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s more than just when I’m hearing something, I think. The voices or the work stuff make it sharper, like it gets louder in my head, but the guilt is kind of there most of the time now.\n\nI keep going back over things, especially from work — like every camera angle I might have missed, every time I hesitated before calling something in, every moment where I think I should’ve noticed more. And I don’t just think, “Oh, that was a mistake.” It turns into, “That proves I’m careless,” or “I’m dangerous to have watching anything important.”\n\nSo when I’m thinking about work, it gets really intense. But even away from work, it lingers. I’ll be doing something else and suddenly I’m back there in my head, replaying it again, trying to figure out what I missed. And if anything goes wrong on my watch, even if nobody has said it was my fault, part of me feels like I deserve to be punished for it. Like I should be held responsible because I should have prevented it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I used to have things I actually looked forward to. Salsa class was a big one — I’d be thinking about it during the week, what shoes to wear, whether I was getting better at a turn, that kind of thing. And softball, too. Even if I was tired, once I got there I’d usually be glad I went.\n\nI used to like building PCs, messing around with parts, watching videos about setups, comparing components — it was this satisfying thing where I could focus and feel like I was making something work. And I’d have people over for board games sometimes, which sounds small, but I really did enjoy setting it up, picking games, making snacks, all of that.\n\nLately I just… don’t answer. People text about salsa or softball or ask if I’m coming, or someone says we should do a game night, and I just let it sit there. I see the message, and I know the old version of me would’ve responded, but now it’s like there’s this wall between me and it. I just stay home. I don’t even really miss it in the normal way — it’s more like none of it can reach me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think that’s fair to say. It’s not just that I’m busy or tired or forgetting to go. It’s more like the part of me that used to want those things has gone quiet.\n\nLike salsa class — I used to actually look forward to it. I’d think about it ahead of time, and even if I was nervous or had a long day, once I was there I could feel myself sort of come alive a little. Same with softball. I wasn’t always amazing or anything, but I liked showing up, being part of it.\n\nAnd building PCs used to really pull me in. I could spend hours comparing parts or watching videos or planning a setup, and it felt satisfying. Now I’ll maybe see something about it online and just scroll past it. It doesn’t hook me.\n\nBoard games too… I used to have people over and enjoy planning it, picking the game, setting snacks out, making it feel like a little event. Now when people text me about getting together, I mostly just ignore it. I see the messages sitting there, and I know I should answer, but I don’t. I just stay home.\n\nIt’s like those things are still there, but they don’t reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy gray mood that you mentioned?\n\nPatient: I think… about four months now. It’s hard to pin down exactly because at first I kept telling myself it was just stress, like I was adjusting, or maybe I was just tired from the change.\n\nBut it really started after I got moved into the lead casino surveillance role. That’s when the gray feeling began to settle in more and more. At first it was maybe just some bad days, but then it became most days, and then it felt like I couldn’t really get out from under it. So… yeah, roughly four months. Since that move happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy doing anything that brings you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. I keep trying to think of something, because it feels strange to say “nothing,” like I’m being dramatic, but the things that used to make me feel like myself just don’t do that anymore.\n\nSalsa used to be a real thing for me — I’d look forward to it, plan around it, get a little nervous in a good way, you know? Softball too, even just being around the team and having a reason to show up somewhere. And I used to love building PCs, like comparing parts and getting absorbed in it for hours. Board game nights were another one… I liked having people over, setting everything up, making snacks, picking the right game.\n\nNow people text me about those things, and I just… leave it. I see the messages. I know they’re there. I’ll even open them sometimes and then close them again. And then I just sit at home. It’s like I can remember that those things used to matter, but I can’t feel it anymore. None of it really reaches me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you've lost interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been about four months, I’d say. I know I mentioned the mood part started around then too, but the losing interest kind of came along with it — maybe not all at once, but that’s when I started noticing I wasn’t reaching for things anymore.\n\nIt was after I got moved into the lead casino surveillance role. At first I thought I was just adjusting or overwhelmed, and I kept telling myself, “Okay, once I get used to the new position I’ll feel like going back to things.” But then weeks kept passing, and I still wasn’t going to salsa, wasn’t showing up for softball, wasn’t getting excited about PC stuff or board game nights.\n\nSo… roughly four months. Since that role change.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in things most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, definitely. It’s been much longer than two weeks. I mean, I almost wish it were just a couple of weeks, because then I could tell myself it was a bad stretch or something temporary, but it’s been about four months now.\n\nIt really started around when I was moved into the lead casino surveillance role. Since then, the depressed feeling and the not caring about things I used to care about have been there most of the day, almost every day. There might be little moments where I’m distracted, but it doesn’t really lift. It’s just been… there, kind of sitting on me, since that change happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you didn't feel depressed or lose interest in activities?\n\nPatient: No… not really. Not because anything specific happened right then. Just… seeing them, knowing I’m going in, feeling that weight.\n\nSo no, in the past two weeks, I can’t think of a day where I felt okay or actually interested in things again. It’s been pretty much every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a significant change in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past four months?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I guess I haven’t been tracking it closely. I don’t think there’s been some dramatic shift that I could point to. I mean… yes, most of every day. It’s not like I wake up neutral and then something happens. Most mornings it’s already there before I even get out of bed, like this heavy gray mood has settled over me overnight and I’m just… starting the day underneath it.\n\nAnd it doesn’t really go away. There are moments where I’m doing something and I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t call that feeling better. It’s more like I’m just moving through it.
